follows and is given in
Section

5.4
.

The first ingredient we need is that boundedness of
B_{t-1}
does imply that the
t
-th player is unable to distinguish between its input being from the
YES
distribution or the
NO
distribution. This is stated as
Lemma

5.18
. Next we need to show that given information about
A_{t,c_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}
, the posterior distribution of
\mathbf{x}^{*}
is indeed bounded, and we assert this in
Lemma

5.17
. Note that this also serves as the base case of our induction. Finally we argue that if
B_{t-1}
is bounded, then for most pairs of matchings
A_{t}
and centers
\mathbf{c}_{t}
the resulting set
B_{t}
is bounded. This is asserted in
Lemma

5.19
. See also
Fig.

1
for a pictorial overview of the proof structure of
Lemma

5.1
.

In the rest of this section, after showing that
Lemma

5.1
implies
Theorem

3.5
in
Section

5.1
, we introduce the posterior sets and discuss their basic properties in
Section

5.2
, we introduce boundedness and state the three lemmas above in
Section

5.3
, and finally conclude with the proof of
Lemma

5.1
in
Section

5.4
.

5.1
Proof of
Theorem

3.5

We now show how the lemma suffices to prove
Theorem 3.5
. The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma 6.3 in
[
KK19
]
. We remark that the lemma is not immediate and effectively depends on the fact that players can jointly sample from the
NO
distribution on their own. (Note the players can’t jointly sample from the
YES
distribution since these samples are correlated by the hidden vector
\mathbf{x}^{*}
. So the proof is inherently asymmetric visavis the treatment of the
YES
and
NO
distributions.)

Proof of
Theorem

3.5
.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that there exists a protocol
\Pi=(r_{1},\dots,r_{T})
that solves IFRMD with advantage more than
\delta
and less than
\tau n
bits of communication for some
n\geq n_{0}
. In what follows, we will show that
\|(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{Y}_{1:T})-(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{N}_{1:T})\|_{tvd}\leq\delta
, which implies that the advantage of the protocol cannot be greater than
\delta
, hence producing a contradiction.

For every
q,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\alpha_{0}>0
as in
Lemma

5.1
.
For every
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
,
T\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\delta^{\prime}=\delta/2
, we set
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau\in(0,1)
as in
Lemma

5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}\supset\mathcal{E}_{2}\supset\cdots\supset\mathcal{E}_{T}
be the sequence of events guaranteed by
5.1
such that
\Pr\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\,|\,\mathcal{E}_{t-1}\right]\leq\delta^{\prime}/T
for
t=2,3\dots,T
. Note that by the properties of these events, with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
, we have
\|S^{Y}_{t}-r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})\|_{tvd}\leq\delta^{\prime}/T
for all
t\in[T]
. We use
\|\cdot\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}
to denote the total variation distance of distributions conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{t}
. We inductively show that for every
t\in[T]
,

\|(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t})-(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{1:t})\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\leq\frac{t\delta^{\prime}}{T}.

First, we prove the base case
t=1
. Recalling that
S_{0}^{Y}=S_{0}^{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{1}^{Y})-(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{1}^{N})\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\|(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{1}^{Y})-(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},r_{1}(M_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{0}^{N},U_{1}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\|(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{1}^{Y})-(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},r_{1}(M_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{0}^{Y},U_{1}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{1}}\,.

Observe that for every fixed
A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1}
and
S^{Y}_{0}
satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have
\|S_{1}^{Y}-r_{1}(M_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{0}^{Y},U_{1})\|_{tvd}\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{T}
, where the randomness is over
S_{1}^{Y}
and
U_{1}
. It follows from
2.3
that

\|(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{1}^{Y})-(A_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},r_{1}(M_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1},S_{0}^{Y},U_{1}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{1}}\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{T},

which completes the base case.

Next, we tackle the induction step. For every
t=2,\dots,T
, we have

\|(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t})-(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{1:t})\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\\
=\|(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{1:t-1},r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{t-1},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\,.

Let us define
Q_{t-1}^{Y}=(A_{1:t-1},\mathbf{c}_{1:t-1},S^{Y}_{1:t-1})
and
Q_{t-1}^{N}=(A_{1:t-1},\mathbf{c}_{1:t-1},S^{N}_{1:t-1})
.
Then, we can rewrite the above expression for total variation distance in terms of the new notation as follows:

\|(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{1:t-1},r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{t-1},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\\
=\|(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(Q_{t-1}^{N},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{N},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\,.

(5.3)

We now apply
2.4
to
Equation 5.3
. Applying this lemma with
X^{1}=Q_{t-1}^{Y}
,
X^{2}=Q_{t-1}^{N}
,
Z^{1}=(A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})
,
Z^{2}=(A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t})
, and
f
as the function that maps the tuple
(X,(B,C))
to
(B,r_{t}(X,B,C))
, we get

\displaystyle\|(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(Q_{t-1}^{N},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{N},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle\|Q_{t-1}^{Y}-Q_{t-1}^{N}\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}+\|(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\|Q_{t-1}^{Y}-Q_{t-1}^{N}\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t-1}}+\|(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\,,

(5.4)

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\mathcal{E}_{t}\subset\mathcal{E}_{t-1}
and condition (i) of
5.1
which states that
\mathcal{E}_{t-1}
only depends on
(A_{1:t-1},\mathbf{c}_{1:t-1})
and
S^{Y}_{1:t-2}
.

Now, by applying the induction hypothesis, we have that

\|Q_{t-1}^{Y}-Q_{t-1}^{N}\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t-1}}\leq\frac{(t-1)\delta^{\prime}}{T}.

(5.5)

Next, we bound the second term on the right hand side of (
5.1
), i.e.,

\|(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}},

by applying condition (iv) from
5.1
. According to this condition, for every
fixed

(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t})
and
S^{Y}_{1:t-1}
satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{t}
, we have

\|r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})\|_{tvd}\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{T},

where
U_{t}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})
. Thus, by
2.3
, it follows that

\|(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}))-(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},r_{t}(Q_{t-1}^{Y},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t},U_{t}))\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{T}\,.

(5.6)

Combining
Eqs.

5.3
,
5.1
,
5.5
and
5.6
, we have

\|(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t})-(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{N}_{1:t})\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{t}}\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}t}{T},

which completes the induction.

Substituting
t=T
, we conclude that

\|(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{Y}_{1:T})-(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{N}_{1:T})\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{T}}\leq\delta^{\prime}.

Finally, by removing the conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{T}
, we have

\displaystyle|(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{Y}_{1:T})-(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{N}_{1:T})\|_{tvd}

\displaystyle\leq|(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{Y}_{1:T})-(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S^{N}_{1:T})\|_{tvd,\mathcal{E}_{T}}+\Pr[\overline{\mathcal{E}_{T}}]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta.

This implies that
\Pi
cannot have advantage more than
\delta
, which contradicts the assumptions of the theorem statement. Therefore, we conclude that any protocol for
IFRMD
with advantage
\delta
requires
\tau n
bits of communication, as desired.
∎

5.2
Posterior sets and functions

The main challenge in proving
5.1
lies in the condition (iv), i.e., requiring the closeness of the Yes message (i.e.,
S^{Y}_{t}=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})
) and the hybrid No message (i.e.,
r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})
).
Intuitively, if
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})
and is independent of the other arguments, then
A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}
is uniformly distributed over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n}
and hence
S^{Y}_{t}
follows the same distribution as
r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})
. However,
\mathbf{x}^{*}
is correlated
6
6

6

In particular,
\mathbf{x}^{*}
has to be consistent with the previous messages
S^{Y}_{1:t-1}
.
with the previous messages
S^{Y}_{1:t-1}
so the above ideal situation would not happen in general. Nevertheless, we are able to analyze the conditional distribution of
A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}
on the previous messages by explicitly characterizing the
posterior distribution
of
\mathbf{x}^{*}
after receiving the messages from the first
t-1
players. That is, the conditional distribution of
A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}
can be described by first sampling
\mathbf{x}^{*}
from the posterior distribution and then applying
A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}
.

For every fixed
A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t}
and
S_{1:t}
, we would like to identify a distribution
\mathcal{D}_{t}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{t}
is the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{x}^{*}
given messages
S_{1:t}
. Note that by the choice of the No case, the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{x}^{*}
given messages
S_{1:t}
is simply the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Thus, we only need to worry about the Yes case.

5.7
Posterior sets and functions.

Under the setting described above, for each
t
and fixed
A_{1:t}
,
\mathbf{c}_{1:t}
, and
S_{1:t}
, define

•

(Reduced posterior set)
B_{r,t}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
be the set of possible values of
z_{t}=A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}
that leads to message
S_{t}
; Note that
B_{r,t}
should be thought of as a function on
A_{t}
,
\mathbf{c}_{t}
, and
S_{t}
in the sense that
B_{r,t}=g_{t}^{-1}(S_{t})
where
g_{t}(\cdot)=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S_{1:t-1},\cdot)
. Let
\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,t}}
be the indicator function of
B_{r,t}
.

•

(Posterior set and function) Let

B_{t}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\,|\,A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r,t}\}\,.

Also, let
\mathbf{1}_{B_{t}}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the indicator function of
B_{t}
.

•

(Aggregated posterior set and function) Let

B_{1:t}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\,|\,A_{t^{\prime},\mathbf{c}_{t^{\prime}}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r,t^{\prime}},\ \forall t^{\prime}=1,\dots,t\}=\bigcap_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t}B_{t^{\prime}}\,.

Also, let
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be the indicator function of
B_{1:t}
. Namely,
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}=\prod_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{B_{t^{\prime}}}
.

Now, we show that
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}
captures the posterior distribution (i.e., the conditional distribution) of
\mathbf{x}
given messages
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{t}
:

5.8
Posterior function
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}
captures the posterior distribution..

For every
t\in[T]
, the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{x}
given messages
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{t-1}
is exactly given by
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}(\mathbf{x})/\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}\|_{1}
.
In particular, for fixed
A_{1:t}
,
\mathbf{c}_{1:t}
, and
S_{1:t-1}^{Y}
, we have
S^{Y}_{t}=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S_{1:t-1}^{Y},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})
, where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us focus on the case of
t=T
. The proof is done by some direct manipulations of Bayes rule.
By definition, the conditional probability of
\mathbf{x}
given messages
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{T}
would be

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}\,\mid\,A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\right]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}\wedge A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\right]}{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\right]}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\,\mid\,\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}\right]\cdot\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}[\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}]}{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\right]}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*}}[\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}]}{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\right]}\cdot\prod_{t=1}^{T}\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\,\mid\,\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{q^{-n}}{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*},A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{r,t}\ \forall t\in[T]\right]}\cdot\prod_{t=1}^{T}\Pr_{A_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t}}\left[A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r,t}\right].

As the above quantity is proportional to
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:T}}(\mathbf{x})
, we conclude that the conditional probability of
\mathbf{x}
given messages
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{T}
is given by
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:T}}(\mathbf{x})/\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:T}}\|_{1}
.

Next, by definition we have
S^{Y}_{T}=r_{T}(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{T}}\mathbf{x}^{*})
where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})
. Thus, the conditional distribution of
S^{Y}_{T}
on
A_{1:T}
,
\mathbf{c}_{1:T}
, and
S_{1:T-1}^{Y}
is

\displaystyle\Pr[S^{Y}_{T}=S\,|\,A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y}]=\frac{\Pr[S^{Y}_{T}=S,A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y}]}{\Pr[A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y}]}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})}[S=r_{T}(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{T}}\mathbf{x}^{*}),A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{t}^{Y}=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S_{1:t-1}^{Y},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})\ \forall t\in[T-1]]}{\Pr[A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y}]}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})}[S=r_{T}(A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{T}}\mathbf{x}^{*}),A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},\mathbf{x}^{*}\in B_{1:T}]}{\Pr[A_{1:T},\mathbf{c}_{1:T},S_{1:T-1}^{Y}]}\,.

Namely,
S^{Y}_{t}=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S_{1:t-1}^{Y},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})
, where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
.
∎

Now that we have a characterization of the posterior distribution of
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, the following corollary shows that
Equation 5.2
(i.e., the condition (iv) of
5.1
) can be simplified to bounding the total variation distance between the posterior distribution and the uniform distribution.

5.9
Reducing
Equation 5.2
.

Let
r_{t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},B_{1:t},U_{t}
be defined as before, we have

\|r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})\|_{tvd}\leq\|(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-U_{t}\|_{tvd}

where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
.

Proof.

By
5.8
, we have

S^{Y}_{t}=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})

where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
. Note that when we fix
A_{1:t}
,
\mathbf{c}_{1:t}
, and
S^{Y}_{1:t-1}
(hence
B_{1:t}
is also fixed), by data processing inequality (see item 2 of
2.2
) we have

\|r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})\|_{tvd}\leq\|(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-U_{t}\|_{tvd}\,.

∎

Namely,
Equation 5.2
(i.e., the condition (iv) of
5.1
) can be replaced with
\|(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-U_{t}\|_{tvd}\leq\gamma/T
, i.e., after applying a random folded hypermatching matrix
A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}
to the posterior distribution
\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
, the distribution of the resulting string is close to the uniform distribution
\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})
.

Finally, the following lemma shows that when the amount of communication is small, the posterior set is large with high probability.

5.10
Posterior set is large.

Let
\Pi=(r_{1},\dots,r_{T})
be a deterministic protocol for
IFRMD
where each message function
r_{t}
outputs a message of length at most
s
bits for some
1\leq s\leq n
. Let
B_{t}
be the posterior set defined in
5.7
for every
t\in[T]
. For every
\delta\in(0,1)
and
t\in[T]
, we have
|B_{t}|\geq\delta\cdot q^{n-s}
with probability at least
1-\delta
over the randomness of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

Proof.

Fix a hypermatching
M
and centers
\mathbf{c}
, the
t
-th message function induces a partition
P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup\cdots\cup P_{2^{s}}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. For each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, we define
P(\mathbf{x})
to be the part that contains
\mathbf{x}
, i.e, if
\mathbf{x}\in P_{i}
, then
P(\mathbf{x})=P_{i}
. Note that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\left[\frac{1}{|P(\mathbf{x})|}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{s}}\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}[\mathbf{x}\in P_{i}]}{|P_{i}|}=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{s}}\frac{|P_{i}|\cdot q^{-n}}{|P_{i}|}=\frac{2^{s}}{q^{n}}\leq q^{s-n}\,.

By Markov’s inequality, we have
|P(\mathbf{x})|<\delta\cdot q^{n-s}
with probability at most
\delta
as desired.
∎

5.3
Fourier analytic conditions

In this subsection, we define and analyze Fourier-analytic properties of the posterior set
B
and show that these properties are sufficient for the condition (iv) (i.e.,
5.9
) of
5.1
.

5.3.1
Three key definitions

Recall that given a matching
M=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m})
and centers
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})
,
A_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
\mathbf{c}
-centered folded encoding of
M
. We are going to define three properties for sets
B
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.
First, we say a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted if
B
is a union of cosets (affine shifts) of the null space of
A_{\mathbf{c}}
.

5.11
Restricted set.

Let
M
be a
k
-hypermatching of size
m
and
\mathbf{c}
be centers. We say a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted if there exists a (“reduced”) set
B_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
such that
B=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\,|\,A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r}\}
.

Note that the posterior set (
Definition

5.7
) of round
t
is
(M_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t})
-restricted.

Next, we introduce the notion of a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
being
(strongly/weakly) bounded
. These notions are similar to those in
[
KK19
, Definition 4.3]
. They say that a set
B
is bounded if the Fourier spectrum of the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{B}
can be appropriately bounded in terms of the
\ell_{1}
norm on various Hamming levels.

First, we introduce some notation. Note that for every set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the
\mathbf{0}
-th Fourier coefficient of the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{B}
is
|B|/q^{n}
. In what follows we study the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{1}_{B}
after scaling by
q^{n}/|B|
so that the
\mathbf{0}
-th Fourier coefficient after scaling has value
1
. In what follows we define weak and strong bounding functions for the
\ell_{1}
norm of the Fourier coefficients based on Hamming weight. Not all functions will satisfy the desired bounds, but indicators of posterior sets turn out to satisfy these bounds and this is the crux of our (and
[
KK19
]
’s) analysis.

W_{C,s}(h):=\begin{cases}1,\quad&h=0\\
\left(\frac{C\sqrt{sn}}{h}\right)^{h/2},&1\leq h\leq s\\
\infty,&h>s.\end{cases}.

(5.12)

U_{C,s}(h):=\begin{cases}W_{C,s}(h),&0\leq h\leq s\\
\min\left\{W_{C,h}(h),\left(\frac{2q^{2}e^{2}n}{h}\right)^{h/2}\right\},&h>s.\end{cases}.

(5.13)

(Above,
U
stands for Upper bound, while
W
stands for a Weak upper bound.)

5.14
(Strongly/weakly) Bounded set.

Let
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq s\leq n
,
C>0
, and
B\subset\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. We say
B
(as well as its indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{B}
) is
(C,s)
-(strongly)-bounded if, for every
h\in[n]
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|\leq U_{C,s}(h).

(5.15)

We say that
B
is
(C,s)
-weakly-bounded if the bound on the RHS above is replaced by
W_{C,s}(h)
. (Unless otherwise specified we use “bounded” to mean “strongly bounded”.)

Remark
.

As we keep track of posterior sets that are inductively refined, we will need the
entire
Fourier spectrum of the corresponding indicator functions to be bounded from above by the function
U_{C,s}
(for appropriate
C,s>0
). The notion of boundedness is such that it allows us to show that
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}
is close to the uniform distribution on
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n}
when
\mathbf{x}
is drawn from a bounded posterior set
B\subset\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
(see
Lemma

5.18
). Our upper bounds typically establish only the weak bound
W_{C,s}(h)
(particularly,
Lemma

6.18
and
Lemma

6.26
), we usually prove this holds for every
s
in some large interval and this allows us along with standard Fourier analysis (see
Lemma

6.5
) to establish the stronger bound for a slightly worse choice of constant
C
.

Finally, in what follows we will show that the intersection of a bounded set with a “restricted set” is also bounded and this will be the core of our induction. To do this we need to understand the Fourier behavior of restricted sets. It turns out that restricted sets satisfy a property stronger than being bounded, which we term “reduced”-ness below.

5.16
(Weakly/Strongly) Reduced set.

Let
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq s\leq n
,
C>0
, and
B\subset\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Let
M
be a
k
-hypermatching. We say
B
(as well as its indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{B}
) is
(M,C,s)
-(strongly)-reduced if the following hold.

•

For every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, if there exists
i\in[n]
such that
u_{i}=1
but
i
is not contained
7
7

7

We use “contained in” and “touched by” interchangeably as in some later contexts it makes more sense to use “touched by” when working with a set of vertices or hyperedges.
in
M
(i.e., none of the hyperedges of
M
contains
i
), then
\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})=0
.

•

For every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, if there exists a hyperedge
e_{i}
of
M
such that
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\bmod{q}
, then
\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})=0
.

•

For every
h\in\{1,\dots,s\}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|\leq U_{C,s}(h)\,.

If the bound in the RHS is replaced by the weaker
W_{C,s}(h)
bound, then we say that
B
is a weakly-reduced set. (Again we usually suppress the word “strongly” and simply refer to strongly-reduced sets as reduced set.)

As a remark, the first two conditions in the definition of reducedness are motivated by the Fourier analytic properties of posterior sets (e.g.,
Lemma

6.1
, and
6.17
). The third condition is a strengthening of boundedness. In particular the third condition applied with
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
implies that every
(M,C,s)
-(strongly/weakly)-reduced set
B
is also
(C,s)
-(strongly/weakly)-bounded.
That is why we say reducedness is the intersection of restrictedness and boundedness.

In summary, restrictedness (
Definition

5.11
) is a certain posterior property and boundedness is a certain Fourier analytic condition while reducedness is the intersection of the two. By
Definition

5.7
, we immediately have that each posterior set
B_{t}
is
(M_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t})
-restricted and in the lemmas stated below we will establish that
B_{t}
is
(M_{t},C_{0},s)
-reduced and the aggregated posterior set
B_{1:t}
is
(C_{t},s)
-bounded with high probability (for some choices of parameters
s,C_{0},C_{1},\dots,C_{T}
). See also
Fig.

1
for a pictorial view of these definitions.

5.3.2
Three key lemmas

There are three key lemmas about these Fourier analytic conditions. The first lemma establishes the base case of the induction toward showing the aggregated posterior set being
(C,s)
-bounded (for some
C=O(1)
and
s=\Omega(n)
). In fact, we show a stronger guarantee in which every posterior set
B_{t}
is
(M_{t},C,s)
-reduced.

5.17
Base case.

For every
q,k\geq 2
, there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
C_{0}<\infty
such that
for every sufficiently large
n
, every
k
-hypermatching
M
on vertex set
[n]
, every pair of integers
b,s
satisfying
0<b\leq s\leq\varepsilon_{0}\cdot n
the following holds. If
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
satisfies (i) there exists a sequence of centers
\mathbf{c}
such that
B
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted, and (ii)
|B|\geq q^{n-b}
, then
B
is
(M,C_{0},s)
-reduced.

The proof of
5.17
is given in
Section

6.3
.

Recall from
5.9
that the condition (iv) in
5.1
is implied by showing
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}
is close to the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
with high probability over the choice of
A_{\mathbf{c}}
where
\mathbf{x}
is sampled uniformly from the posterior set
B_{1:t}
. The second key lemma shows that
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}^{*}
is indeed close to uniform when the posterior set is bounded.

5.18
Boundedness implies closeness to uniformity.

For every
q,k\geq 2
there exists
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(k,q)
such that for every
\delta\in(0,1/2)
and
C<\infty
, there exists
\tau=\tau(q,k,\delta,C)
such that the following holds for every sufficiently large
n
:

Let
B\subset\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
be a
(C,s)
-bounded set with
|B|\geq q^{n-b}
, for
4\log_{q}(3/\delta)\leq b\leq s\leq\tau n
. Let
M
be a random
k
-hypermatching of size
m\leq\alpha_{0}n
and
\mathbf{c}
be a uniformly random sequence of centers for
M
and let
A_{\mathbf{c}}
denote the
\mathbf{c}
centered folded encoding of
M
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
over the choice of
M
and
\mathbf{c}
, for every
\mathbf{z}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
, we have that

1-\delta<q^{(k-1)m}\Pr_{\mathbf{x}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B)}[A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}_{0}]<1+\delta\,.

As a consequence, we also have (with probability at least
1-\delta
over the choice of
(M,\mathbf{c})
):

1.

\|(A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x})-U\|_{tvd}\leq\delta
where
\mathbf{x}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B)
and
U\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m})
.

2.

For every non-negative function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
,

(1-\delta)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B)}\left[f(A_{c}\mathbf{x})\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m})}\left[f(\mathbf{z})\right]}\leq(1+\delta)\,.

The proof of
Lemma

5.18
is postponed to
Section

6.4
.

Our final lemma of this section asserts that if
\mathbf{1}_{B_{1:t}}
is
(C,s)
-bounded, then
f_{1:t+1}
is
(O(C),s)
-bounded with high probability.

5.19
Induction step.

For every
q,k\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
\alpha_{0}>0
and
C_{0}>0
such that for every
C\geq C_{0}
, and
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, there exist
\tau_{0}\in(0,1)
and
C^{\prime}>0

such that the following holds.
For every
n,b,b^{\prime},s,m\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying
m\leq\alpha_{0}n
,
0<b,b^{\prime},s<\tau_{0}n
and every
(C,s)
-bounded set
B\subset\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
satisfying
|B|\geq q^{n-b}
, we have that with probability at least
1-4\delta
over a uniformly random
k
-hypermatching
M
of size at most
m
and every
(M,C_{0},s)
-reduced set
B^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
satisfying
|B^{\prime}|\geq q^{n-b^{\prime}}
and
|B\cap B^{\prime}|\geq(1-\delta)\cdot|B|\cdot|B^{\prime}|/q^{n}\geq q^{n-s}
, we have
B\cap B^{\prime}
is
(C^{\prime},s)
-bounded.

Lemma

5.19
is proved in
Section

6.5
. In our inductive application of the lemma above,
we set
B\leftarrow B_{1:t-1}
and
B^{\prime}\leftarrow B_{t}
for every
t\in\{2,3,\dots,T\}
to get that all the
B_{t}
’s are bounded and this is the core of the proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

5.4
Proof of Lemma
5.1

Figure 1:
A pictorial overview of the proof of
5.1
. Each posterior set
B_{t}
(the blue sets) is both
(M_{t},\mathbf{c}_{t})
-restricted (followed from
Definition

5.7
) and
(M_{t},C_{0},s)
-reduced (followed from
Lemma

5.17
). Each aggregated posterior set
B_{1:t}
(the orange sets) is
(C_{t},s)
-bounded (followed from
Lemma

5.19
).

Proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

Overview of proof:

(See
Fig.

1
for a pictorial overview of the proof.) The rough overview of the proof is to show that for an appropriate choice of the constants
C_{1},\ldots,C_{T}
, for every
t\in[T]
, the posterior set
B_{1:t}
is
(C_{t},s)
-bounded. Once we have this, we can apply “boundedness implies uniformity” lemma (
Lemma

5.18
) to conclude that the messages sent by the
t
th player on the YES and NO distributions are indistinguishable. To show the boundedness condition for
B_{1:t}
we use induction to deduce that
B_{1:t-1}
is bounded, and then reason about
B_{t}
to conclude that it is large,
(M,C_{0},s)
-reduced (for appropriate
C_{0}
), and crucially that it is roughly independent of
B_{1:t-1}
. Proving the former parts uses
Lemma

5.17
whereas the last part involves another application of the boundedness implies uniformity lemma (on
B_{1:t-1}
). With these ingredients in place the induction step lemma (
Lemma

5.19
) yields the boundedness of
B_{1:t}
. We give the details below.

Setting of parameters:

We note that in addition to the parameters
\alpha_{0}
,
\tau
and
n_{0}
required by the lemma statement, we also need to specify the constants
C_{t}
alluded in the overview. Additionally we also specify three integer parameters:
s^{*}
which specifies the length of the message,
b
which quantifies largeness of various posterior sets, and
s
which quantifies the boundedness of posterior functions.

Given
q
and
k
, let
\alpha_{0}^{1}
be the
\alpha_{0}(k,q)
from
Lemma

5.18
and
\alpha_{0}^{2}
be the
\alpha_{0}(k,q)
from
Lemma

5.19
. We set
\alpha_{0}=\min\{\alpha_{0}^{1},\alpha_{0}^{2}\}
. Let
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(k,q)
be the constant from
Lemma

5.17
. Now given
T
and
\delta
, we need to specify
\tau>0
and
n_{0}<\infty
. We first set a large number of intermediate parameters that will be used in the rest of the proof. Recall that
Lemma

5.19
takes as input parameters
q,k,\delta
and
C\geq C_{0}
and gives constants
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(q,k,\delta,C)
and
\tau_{0}(q,k,\delta,C)
for which the lemma holds.
We let
\delta^{\prime}=\min\{\frac{1}{2},\delta/(10T)\}
. We set
C_{1}=C_{0}(q,k)
to be the constant from
Lemma

5.17
. For
t\in\{2,\ldots,T\}
we set
C_{t}=C^{\prime}(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t-1})
where
C^{\prime}(\cdots)
is the aforementioned function from
Lemma

5.19
. Next for every
t\in[T]
we set
\gamma_{t}=\tau_{0}(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t})
. Further, let
\tau(q,k,\delta,C)
be the function from
Lemma

5.18
. For
t\in[T]
, let
\rho_{t}=\tau(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{y})
. Let
\zeta=\min\{\varepsilon_{0},\min_{t\in[T]}\{\gamma_{t}\},\min_{t\in[T]}\{\rho_{t}\}\}
. Let
\nu=\zeta/(2T)
and let
\tau=\nu/2
.
Finally we let
n_{0}=\max\{\frac{2}{\nu}\log_{q}(1/\delta^{\prime}),\frac{4}{\nu}\log_{q}(3/\delta^{\prime})\}
.
8
8

8

The reader may notice that one of these terms obviously dominates the other and we could have simplified this expression. But we keep them separate for easier verifiability in the proof. We follow this practice through most of this paper.
Finally, given
n\geq n_{0}
we set
s=\zeta n
,
b=\nu n
and
s^{*}\leq\tau n
.

Note that these settings ensure
b\geq 1
,
s^{*}\leq b/2\leq b-\log_{q}(1/\delta^{\prime})
,
2tb\leq s\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
for every
t\in[T]
,
s\leq\tau_{t}n=\tau_{0}(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t})n
for every
t\in[T]
and
4\log_{q}(3/\delta^{\prime})\leq 2tb\leq s\leq\rho_{t}n=\tau(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t})n
for every
t\in[T]
. These inequalities will be used in the proof below,

The events
\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{T}
:

Recall the notion of posterior sets
B_{t}
and aggregate posterior sets
B_{1:t}
for
t\in[T]
from
Definition

5.7
.
We define
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{1}
to be the event that
|B_{1}|\geq q^{n-2b}
and
B_{1}
is
(C_{1},s)\text{-bounded}
. For
t\in\{2,3,\dots,T\}
, let
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}
denote the event that
B_{t}
is
(M_{t},C_{0},s)
-reduced and the aggregated posterior set
B_{1:t}
is
(C_{t},s)
-bounded and large i.e.,
|B_{1:t}|\geq q^{n-2tb}
.
Next, for
t\in[T]
, we define
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{t}
to be the event that
\|A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*}-U_{t}\|_{tvd}\leq\delta^{\prime}
, where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
and
U_{t}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})
. Finally we let
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}^{1}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}^{2}
, and for
t\in\{2,3,\dots,T\}
, we let
\mathcal{E}_{t}=\mathcal{E}_{t-1}\cap\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}_{t}
. We now turn to proving conditions (i)-(iv) hold for this choice of events.

Causality and Indistinguishability:

It is immediate from the definition that
\mathcal{E}_{1}\supset\mathcal{E}_{2}\supset\cdots\supset\mathcal{E}_{T}
and
\mathcal{E}_{t}
only depends on
A_{1:t}
and
\mathbf{c}_{1:t}
. This establishes condition (i).

To verify condition (iv), note that by definition of
\mathcal{E}_{t}
we have that
\|(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-U_{t}\|_{tvd}\leq\delta^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
and
U_{t}\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})
. As
S_{1:t}^{Y}=r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})
where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t})
, by the data processing inequality we have
\|S^{Y}_{t}-r_{t}(A_{1:t},\mathbf{c}_{1:t},S^{Y}_{1:t-1},U_{t})\|_{tvd}\leq\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta/T
as desired. This proves condition (iv).

Probability of bad events:

By the setting
s^{*}=\tau n\leq b-\log_{q}(1/\delta^{\prime})
we get, by the “posterior set is large” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.10
), that
|B_{1}|\geq q^{n-b}\geq q^{n-2b}

with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
. Now since
B_{1}
is
(M_{1},\mathbf{c}_{1})
-restricted (by definition) and using
2b\leq s\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
we can apply the “base case” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.17
) to conclude
B_{1}
is
(M,C_{1},s)
-reduced where
C_{1}=C_{0}(q,k)
for
C_{0}(\cdots)
being the function from the base case lemma. Thus, by the remark after
Definition

5.16
,
B_{1}
is also
(C_{1},s)
-bounded. This establishes

\Pr[\overline{\mathcal{E}^{1}_{1}}]\leq\delta^{\prime}
.

We now analyze
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}
for
t>1
(and return to
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{t}
’s, including
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{1}
, later). We show that, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{t-1}
,
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}
holds with probability at least
1-6\delta^{\prime}
. The main part of it is proving that
B_{1:t}
is large, which we do in the claim below. (Proving boundedness is then a straightforward application of
Lemma

5.19
, as we show later.)

5.20
.

Let
M_{t}
and
\mathbf{c}_{t}
be chosen uniformly conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{t}
. Then with probability at least
(1-\delta^{\prime})
the posterior set
B_{1:t}
satisfies

|B_{1:t}|\geq(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot|B_{1:t-1}|\cdot|B_{t}|/q^{n}\,.

Proof.

By the event
\mathcal{E}_{t-1}
holding we have that
B_{1:t-1}
is
(C_{t-1},s)
-bounded and hence using the conditions
\tau\leq\rho_{t-1}:=\tau_{0}(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t-1})
and
4\log_{q}(3/\delta^{\prime})\leq b\leq s\leq\tau_{t-1}n=\tau(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t-1})n
, by Part (2) of the “boundedness implies uniformity” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.18
) we have

(1-\delta^{\prime})\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t-1})}\left[f(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}x)\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})}\left[f(z)\right]}\leq(1+\delta^{\prime})\,

(5.21)

for every non-negative function
f
over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n}
, with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
(over the choice of
M_{t}
and
\mathbf{c}_{t}
).
Setting
f
to be the indicator function of
B_{r,t}
(recall that
B_{r,t}
is the “reduced posterior set” from
Definition

5.7
) and applying
Eq.

5.21
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t-1})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,t}}(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}x)\right]=\frac{|B_{1:t}|}{|B_{1:t-1}|}\,,

and

\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,t}}(z)\right]=\frac{|B_{r,t}|}{q^{(k-1)\alpha n}}=\frac{|B_{t}|}{q^{n}}\,.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{|B_{1:t}|}{q^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|B_{1:{t-1}}|}{q^{n}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\textsf{Unif}(B_{1:t-1})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,t}}(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}x)\right]

\displaystyle\geq(1-\delta^{\prime})\frac{|B_{1:{t-1}}|}{q^{n}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\textsf{Unif}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\alpha n})}\left[\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,t}}(z)\right]

\displaystyle=(1-\delta^{\prime})\frac{|B_{1:{t-1}}|}{q^{n}}\cdot\frac{|B_{t}|}{q^{n}}\,.

∎

To use the claim above we first analyze
|B_{t}|
. By the “posterior set is large” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.10
) we have
|B_{t}|\geq q^{n-b}
(again using
s\leq b-\log_{q}(1/\delta^{\prime})
). When
B_{t}
is large, then by the “base case” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.17
) we have
B_{t}
is
(M_{t},C_{0},s)
-reduced using
b\leq s\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
. Furthermore if
|B_{t}|\geq q^{n-b}
, then combined with the inductive bound that
|B_{1:t-1}|\geq q^{n-2(t-1)b}
(implied by
\mathcal{E}_{t-1}
),
5.20
implies

|B_{1:t}|=|B_{1:t-1}\cap B_{t}|\geq(1-\delta^{\prime})\cdot|B_{1:t-1}|\cdot|B_{t}|/q^{n}\geq(1-\delta^{\prime})q^{n-(2t-2)b}q^{-b}\geq q^{n-2tb}\,,

where the final inequality uses the very crude (but true) inequality
1-\delta^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{2}\geq q^{-b}
.

Conditioned on
B_{t}
being large and reduced, we can finally invoke the “induction step” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.19
) with
B=B_{1:t-1}
and
B^{\prime}=B_{t}
with
C=C_{t-1}
to get that
B_{1:t}=B_{1:t-1}\cap B_{t}
is
(C_{t},s)
-bounded with probability at least
1-4\delta^{\prime}
where we use
C_{t}=C^{\prime}(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t-1})
. We note this application requires
\max\{b,2(t-1)b,s\}\leq\gamma_{t-1}n:=\tau_{0}(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t-1})n
which is ensured by our setting of parameters.

Taking the union bound over the three error events, namely (a)
B_{t}
not being large, (b)
B_{1:t}
not being large conditioned on
B_{t}
being large and (c)
B_{1:t}
not being bounded condition on being large, we get that
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}
holds with probability at least
1-6\delta^{\prime}
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{t-1}
.

Finally we turn to bounding
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{t}
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}
. Since
|B_{1:t}|\geq q^{n-2tb}
and
B_{1:t}
is
(C_{t},s)
-bounded, we apply
Lemma

5.18
to analyze
\|(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-U_{t}\|_{tvd}
. The application requires
4\log_{q}(3/\delta^{\prime})\leq 2tb\leq s\leq\rho_{t}n:=\tau(q,k,\delta^{\prime},C_{t})n
which we do have with our setting of parameters.
We conclude that
\|(A_{t,\mathbf{c}_{t}}\mathbf{x}^{*})-U_{t}\|_{tvd}\leq\delta^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
for every fixing of
A_{1:t-1,\mathbf{c}_{1,t-1}}
and
S_{1:t-1}^{Y}
(over the choice of
M_{t}
and
\mathbf{c}_{t}
). We thus have that
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{t}
holds with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
condition on
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{t}
.

Putting the two together we get for every
t\in[T]
(including
t=1
), we have
\mathcal{E}_{t}
holds with probability at least
1-7\delta^{\prime}=1-7\delta/(10T)\geq 1-\delta/T
as required for conditions (ii) and (iii).

This completes the proof of
5.1
.

∎

6
Analysis of bounded functions

In this section, we provide the complete proof for the three important lemmas in
Section

5
: the “base case” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.17
), the “boundedness implies uniformity” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.18
), and the “induction step” lemma (i.e.,
Lemma

5.19
).
We first establish useful structure on the Fourier coefficients of restricted sets (posterior set is a special case of restricted set) in
Section 6.1
. Next, we prove useful properties for the Fourier analytic conditions in
Section 6.2
. Finally, we prove the three lemmas in
Section

6.3
,
Section

6.4
, and
Section

6.5
respectively.

6.1
Fourier coefficients of the posterior function

Given a
k
-hypermatching
M=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m})
and centers
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})
we say that a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted if there exists a (“reduced”) set
B_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
such that
B=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}|A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r}\}
, where
A_{\mathbf{c}}
is the
\mathbf{c}
-centered folded encoding of
M
. In this section we aim to prove that large restricted sets are bounded.
Recall that given a
k
-hypermatching
M=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m})
on vertex set
[n]
with
m=\alpha n
edges and sequence of centers
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})
with
c_{i}\in e_{i}\subseteq[n]
, the
\mathbf{c}
-centered folded representation of
M
was denoted
A_{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m\times n}
. We say that a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted if there exists a (“reduced”) set
B_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
such that
B=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}|A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r}\}
. For our next lemma we will also need a variant of this matrix named the
\mathbf{c}
-centered projection induced by
M
, which we denote
\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m\times n}
, which is simply the matrix
A_{\mathbf{c}}
with the columns corresponding to
c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}
zeroed out. (In
A_{\mathbf{c}}
each of these columns has
(k-1)

-1
’s. See
Figure 2
.) With this definition in place we can now relate the Fourier coefficients of the indicator of a restricted set to its image.

Figure 2:
An example of
A_{\mathbf{c}},\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}},A^{(1)}_{\mathbf{c}},\tilde{A}^{(1)}_{\mathbf{c}}
with
m=1
,
n=7
,
k=4
,
e_{1}=\{1,3,5,7\}
and
c_{1}=\{5\}
.

Recall that we use
\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to denote the indicator vector of hyperedge
e_{i}
(see
Section 3
).

6.1
Fourier coefficients of the posterior function.

Let
M
be a
k
-hypermatching of size
m
and
\mathbf{c}
be a sequence of centers. Let
A_{\mathbf{c}}
be the folded representation of
M
and
\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}
be the projection induced by
M
. Furthermore, let
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
be an
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted set with
B_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
satisfying
B=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}|A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r}\}
. Let
\mathbf{1}_{B}
denote the indicator function of
B
. Then for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
we have:

\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})=\begin{cases}0,\quad&\text{if $\mathbf{u}$ contains a node not in $M$.}\\
0,\quad&\text{if $\exists i\in[m]$ such that $\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\pmod{q}$}\\
\widehat{\mathbf{1}}_{B_{r}}(\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{u}),\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}.

Proof.

From the definition of the Fourier coefficient we have
\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})=\tfrac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{B}(\mathbf{x})\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}
where
\omega=e^{2\pi i/q}
being the primitive
q
-th root of unity. Using the fact that
B
is restricted, we get

\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{B}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{B_{r}}(\mathbf{z})\cdot\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in B_{r}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}.

We now fix
\mathbf{z}\in B_{r}
and explore the final term
\sum_{{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}}}\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}
. Let
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{z}^{(m)})
where
\mathbf{z}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k-1}
. Also let
A_{\mathbf{c}}^{(1)},\ldots,A_{\mathbf{c}}^{(m)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)\times n}
denote the blocks of
A_{\mathbf{c}}
corresponding to the
m
edges. Now, think of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
as a free module over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and consider the direct sum decomposition
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}=W^{(0)}\oplus\cdots\oplus W^{(m)}
where for
i\in[m]
,
W^{(i)}
is the sub-module of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
generated by
e_{i}
,
W^{(0)}
is the sub-module generated by
[n]-(\cup_{i}e_{i})
, and “
\oplus
” denotes the direct sum of modules. Let us write
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{(0)}+\cdots+\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
where for
i\in\{0,\ldots,m\}
,
\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\in W^{(i)}
.
Similarly write
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{(0)}+\cdots+\mathbf{u}^{(m)}
.
Since
(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(j)}=0
if
i\neq j
we have
\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}
.
Note also that
\mathbf{z}=A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}
if and only if
\mathbf{z}^{(i)}=A_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}
for every
i\in[m]
. Using this notation, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{(0)}\in W^{(0)}}\omega^{(\mathbf{u}^{(0)})^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(0)}}\right)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\in W^{(i)}\\
A_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}=\mathbf{z}^{(i)}\end{subarray}}\omega^{(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}}\right)\,.

Now note that if
\mathbf{u}^{(0)}=0
then the first term is
|W^{(0)}|=q^{n-km}
, else it is zero. Similarly for
i\in[m]
, there are exactly
q
vectors
\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\in W^{(i)}
such that
A_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}=\mathbf{z}^{(i)}
(which are additive shifts of each other on coordinates in
e_{i}
). Concretely, these two solutions are of the form
(\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{z}^{(i)}+a^{k}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. So we have

\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\in W^{(i)}:A_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}=\mathbf{z}^{(i)}}\omega^{(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(i)}}=\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\omega^{(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}(\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{z}^{(i)}+(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}a^{k}}=\omega^{(\mathbf{u}^{(i)})^{\top}(\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{z}^{(i)}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\omega^{a\cdot\|\mathbf{u}^{(i)}\|_{1}}\,.

Moreover,

\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\omega^{a\cdot\|\mathbf{u}^{(i)}\|_{1}}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\|\mathbf{u}^{(i)}\|_{1}\not\equiv 0\bmod{q}\\
q&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}.

Putting all the above together we get

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{z}}}\omega^{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{if $\mathbf{u}$ contains a node not in $M$.}\\
0\quad&\text{if $\exists i\in[m]$ such that $\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\bmod{q}$}\\
q^{n-(k-1)m}\cdot\omega^{(\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{u})^{\top}\mathbf{z}}\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}.

Summing up over all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
and normalizing yields the lemma.

∎

6.2
Basic properties of large weakly-bounded sets

In this section, we relate weakly bounded sets to strongly bounded ones, and also show that the notion of restricted-ness of sets is independent of the choice of centers. These help us prove boundedness in the base case.

We start by stating an immediate consequence of Parseval’s lemma applied to our indicator functions.

6.2
.

For every
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
we have
\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{v})^{2}\leq|B|/q^{n}
.

(
Lemma

6.2
follows from
Lemma

2.6
by noticing that
\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{B}(\mathbf{a})=|B|
.)
Recall that the
(C,s)
-bounded criterion bounds the sum of Fourier coefficients with a fixed weight at most
s
. As we also need to bound the sum of Fourier coefficients of high weight, this can be guaranteed from Parseval’s inequality as shown in the following lemma.

6.3
.

Suppose
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
satisfies
|B|\geq q^{n-b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for every
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and
b<h\leq n
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|\leq\left(\frac{2q^{2}e^{2}n}{h}\right)^{h/2}.

Proof.

From
Lemma

6.2
, we have that

\sum_{\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|^{2}\leq|B|/q^{n}.

Using

\left|\left\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\,|\,\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\right\}\right|\leq(q-1)^{h}\cdot\binom{n}{h}\leq\left(\frac{qen}{h}\right)^{h}

and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sqrt{(|B|/q^{n})\cdot(qen/h)^{h}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(q^{n}/|B|)\cdot(qen/h)^{h}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{q^{b}(qen/h)^{h}}

\displaystyle(\because|B|\geq q^{n-b})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{q^{h}(qen/h)^{h}}

\displaystyle(\because h>b)

\displaystyle\leq(2q^{2}e^{2}n/h)^{h/2}.

∎

Next, we note a basic monotonicity property of the notion of boundedness which will be useful in the future. Recall the function
W_{C,s}(\cdot)
used in the notion of
weakly-bounded
sets from
Definition

5.14
.

6.4
Monotonicity of boundedness.

The following monotonicities hold for
W
and
U
.

1.

If
h\leq s\leq s^{\prime}
then
W_{C,s}(h)\leq W_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
.

2.

If
s\leq s^{\prime}
then for every
h
,
U_{C,s}(h)\leq U_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
. Consequently, if a set
B
is
(C,s)
-(strongly-)bounded then it is also
(C,s^{\prime})
-(strongly-)bounded.

3.

If
C>e
, then
W_{C,s}(h)
and
U_{C,s}(h)
are monotonically increasing in
h\in[1,s]
.

Proof.

The first two monotonicities are definitional, whereas the third one requires some calculations. Details are provided below.

1.

The inequality holds trivially for
h=0
. For
1\leq h\leq s\leq s^{\prime}
, we have that
W_{C,s}(h)=(C\sqrt{sn}/h)^{h/2}\leq(C\sqrt{s^{\prime}n}/h)^{h/2}=W_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
.

2.

Here we consider three possible ranges for
h
. For
h\leq s\leq s^{\prime}
,
U_{C,s}(h)=W_{C,s}(h)
and
U_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)=W_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
and the inequality follows from Item (1). For
h>s^{\prime}
, we have
U_{C,s}(h)=\min\{W_{C,h}(h),(2q^{2}e^{2}n/h)^{h/2}\}=U_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
yielding the desired inequality as an equality.
For
s<h\leq s^{\prime}
, we have
U_{C,s}(h)=\min\{W_{C,h}(h),(2q^{2}e^{2}n/h)^{h/2}\}\leq W_{C,h}(h)\leq W_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)=U_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
, where the second inequality again follows from the Item (1). Thus in all cases we have
U_{C,s}(h)\leq U_{C,s^{\prime}}(h)
and so if a set
B
is
(C,s)
-(strongly-)bounded then it is also
(C,s^{\prime})
-(strongly-)bounded.

3.

Recall that the function
f(x)=x^{1/x}
is decreasing in the interval
(e,\infty)
(since
f^{\prime}(x)=x^{1/x}\cdot\frac{1-\ln x}{x^{2}}
is negative for
x>e
). Note that for
h\in[1,s]
,
W_{C,s}(h)=U_{C,s}(h)=x^{\frac{C\sqrt{sn}}{2x}}
for
x=\frac{C\sqrt{sn}}{h}
. Note that
x
is a strictly decreasing function of
h
. Moreover, for
h\leq s
, we have
x\geq\frac{C\sqrt{sn}}{s}\geq C>e
. Hence, it follows from monotonically decreasing property of
f
that
W_{C,s}(h)
and
U_{C,s}(h)
are monotonically increasing in the described interval, as desired.

∎

We now show how weak boundedness of a large set in an entire regime of
s
implies it is strongly bounded.

6.5
From weak-boundedness to strong-boundedness.

For every
q,C
, and
\varepsilon_{0}
there exists
C^{\prime}
s.t. for all
n
and
s\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
the following holds:
If
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
with
|B|\geq q^{n-s}
is
(C,s^{\prime})
-weakly-bounded for every
s\leq s^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
then
B
is
(C^{\prime},s)
-strongly bounded. Similarly, if
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
with
|B|\geq q^{n-s}
is
(C,s^{\prime})
-weakly-reduced for every
s\leq s^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
then
B
is
(C^{\prime},s)
-strongly reduced.

Proof.

We prove the lemma for
C^{\prime}:=\max\{C,2q^{2}e^{2}/\varepsilon_{0}^{1/2}\}
. We prove the reducedness condition (and the boundedness follows similarly). Fix
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
with
|B|\geq q^{n-s}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Let

\mathrm{wt}(h):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|,

and let
\tilde{W}(h)=(2q^{2}e^{2}n/h)^{h/2}
.
Our goal is to prove that for
h\leq s
,
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq U_{C^{\prime},s}(h)=W_{C^{\prime},s}(h)
, and for
h>s
that
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq\min\{W_{C^{\prime},h}(h),\tilde{W}(h)\}
. For
h\leq s
, by the fact that
B
is
(C,s)
-weakly-reduced, we have
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq W_{C,s}(h)\leq W_{C^{\prime},s}(h)
where the second inequality follows from the definition of
W_{C,s}
which is monotone in
C
. For every
h>s
we have that
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq\tilde{W}(h)
by
Lemma

6.3
and so it suffices to prove that
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq W_{C^{\prime},h}(h)
for every
h>s
. For
h\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
we use that
B
is
(C,s^{\prime})
-weakly-reduced for
s^{\prime}=h
(this is ok since
s\leq s^{\prime}=h\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
) to conclude that
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq W_{C,h}(h)\leq W_{C^{\prime},h}(s)
. For
h>\varepsilon_{0}n
we note that

W_{C^{\prime},h}(h)=(C^{\prime}\sqrt{hn}/h)^{h/2}=(C^{\prime}\sqrt{n/h})^{h/2}\geq(2q^{2}e^{2}\sqrt{n/\varepsilon_{0}h})^{h/2}\geq(2q^{2}e^{2}n/h)^{h/2}=\tilde{W}(h)\,,

where the first inequality uses
C^{\prime}\geq(2q^{2}e^{2}/\varepsilon_{0}^{1/2})
and the next inequality uses
h>\varepsilon_{0}n
.

Thus in this case we have
\mathrm{wt}(h)\leq\tilde{W}(h)\leq W_{C^{\prime},h}(h)
as desired, concluding the proof that
B
is
(C^{\prime},s)
-strongly-reduced.
∎

Finally we show that the notion of a set being restricted is independent of the choice of centers. Recall the definition of set being restricted from
Definition

5.11
.

6.6
Recentering.

Let
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})
and
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=(c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{m})
be two sequences of centers for the same matching
M
. Then a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is

(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted if and only if it is
(M,\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
-restricted.

Proof.

Let
e_{i}^{(t)}=((e_{i}^{(t)})_{1},\dots,(e_{i}^{(t)})_{k}=c_{t})
(for
t=1,2,\dots,m
) be the ordering of hyperedges corresponding to centering
\mathbf{c}
, and let
{e^{\prime}}_{i}^{(t)}=(({e^{\prime}}_{i}^{(t)})_{1},\dots,({e^{\prime}}_{i}^{(t)})_{k}=c^{\prime}_{t})
.

Given a permutation
\pi:[k]\to[k]
, let
P_{\pi}
be a
(k-1)\times(k-1)
matrix defined as follows: For
1\leq i,j\leq k-1
, let

(P_{\pi})_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $j=\pi(i)$}\,,\\
-1&\text{if $j=\pi(k)$}\,,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

For
t=1,2,\dots,m
, let
\pi_{t}:[k]\to[k]
be the permutation defined by
({e^{\prime}}_{i}^{(t)})_{j}=(e_{i}^{(t)})_{\pi(j)}
, and let
\pi^{\prime}_{t}:[k]\to[k]
be the permutation defined by
(e_{i}^{(t)})_{j}=({e^{\prime}}_{i}^{(t)})_{\pi(j)}
. Then, it is not hard to see that
A_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}=Q\cdot A_{\mathbf{c}}
and
A_{\mathbf{c}}=Q^{\prime}\cdot A_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}

where

Q=\begin{pmatrix}P_{\pi_{1}}&&&\\
&P_{\pi_{2}}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&P_{\pi_{m}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\qquad Q^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}P_{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}&&&\\
&P_{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&P_{\pi^{\prime}_{m}}\end{pmatrix},

and
QQ^{\prime}=Q^{\prime}Q=I
, the
(k-1)m\times(k-1)m
identity matrix.

Now, suppose
B\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted. Let
B_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
be the corresponding reduced set satisfying
B=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\mid A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r}\}
. Then, let
B_{r}^{\prime}=\{Q\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{y}\in B_{r}\}
.

Note that if
\mathbf{x}\in B
, then
A_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}=Q(A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x})\in B_{r}^{\prime}
. Similarly, if
A_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r}^{\prime}
, then there is some
\mathbf{y}\in B_{r}
such that
A_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}=Q\mathbf{y}
, and so,
A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}=Q^{\prime}A_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}=Q^{\prime}Q\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}\in B_{r}
, implying that
\mathbf{x}\in B
. It follows that
B
is
(M,\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
-restricted with reduced set
B^{\prime}_{r}
.

In an analogous fashion, it follows that if
B
is
(M,\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
-restricted, then
B
is also
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted. This completes the proof.
∎

6.3
Proof of the “base case” lemma

In this subsection, we prove the “base case” lemma (
Lemma

5.17
), which shows that every posterior set
B_{t}
is
(M_{t},C,s)
-reduced for some constant
C
. We include the statement again below for convenience.

See
5.17

To see how the above lemma connects to posterior sets, think of
B
as
B_{t}
,
M
as
M_{t}
, and
\mathbf{c}
as
\mathbf{c}_{t}
. Note that condition (i) of
Lemma

5.17
holds by the definition of
B_{t}
. As for condition (ii), it holds when the message
S_{t}
is
typical
and we know by averaging argument that this is the case with high probability (see
5.10
for more details).

We now turn to the proof of Lemma
5.17
. The overall proof follows the outline of
[
KK19
]
, but we require extra care in our case, and the proof crucially depends on the ability to recenter (
6.6
) and a slightly more careful probabilistic analysis.

Proof of
Lemma

5.17
.

Let
\zeta
be the constant from
Lemma

2.12
. Let
\zeta_{1}=\max\{1,\zeta\}
. Given
k
and
q
, let
\varepsilon_{0}=\min\{1,k/(8\zeta_{1})\}
. Further let
C_{1}=\sqrt{2^{7/2}\zeta_{1}^{1/2}ek^{3/2}q^{2k}}
and
C_{2}=(2^{8}\zeta_{1}e^{2}kq^{2k})^{1/2}
and
C=\max\{2,C_{1}+C_{2}\}
. For this choice of
C
and
\varepsilon_{0}
let
C^{\prime}
be the constant given by
Lemma

6.5
. We prove the lemma for
C_{0}=C^{\prime}
.

Let
M
be a hypermatching with
m
edges. (Note we must have
m\leq n/k
.)
Recall the definition of
(M,C,s)
-reducedness (
Definition

5.16
). The first two conditions of
(M,C,s)
-reducedness are immediate corollaries of
Lemma

6.1
. In the rest of the proof we focus on showing for every
b\leq s\leq\varepsilon_{0}n
and every
h\in\{1,\dots,s\}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|\leq W_{C,s}(h)\,.

Since this holds for every
s\in[b,\varepsilon_{0}n]
, by
Lemma

6.5
, we get that there is a
C^{\prime}
such that
B
is
(C^{\prime},s)
-reduced for every
s\in[b,\varepsilon_{0}n]
and this yields the lemma given the bound above.

Fix an arbitrary
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. For each
h\in\{1,\dots,s\}
, let
S_{h}=S_{\mathbf{v},h}=\{\mathbf{u}\,\colon\,\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\}
, i.e., the set of Fourier coefficients in the LHS of the above inequality. We prune
S_{h}
to eliminate some terms that are zero. Recall by
Lemma

6.1
that

\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{w})=0
if
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{w})\not\subseteq\textsf{supp}(M)
, or if there exists
i\in[m]
such that
\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\bmod{q}
.
Let

T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}=\{\mathbf{u}\in S_{\mathbf{v},h}\,|\,\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u})\subseteq\textsf{supp}(M),\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\equiv 0\bmod{q}\ \forall i\in[m]\},

denote the resulting set of vectors which includes all non-zero Fourier coefficients. Roughly, our approach below is to (1) give an upper bound on the size of the set
T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}
and (2) bound the sum of the squares of the coefficients in this set. Once we have both these bounds, we can use the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality to conclude the desired bound. Before we undertake these steps, we make some simplifications and some refinements.

Step 0: Regular condition of
\mathbf{v}
.

First note that we can assume
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{v})\subseteq\textsf{supp}(M)
. If this is not the case, consider the vector
\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}
given by
\widetilde{v_{i}}=v_{i}
if
i\in\textsf{supp}(M)
and
\widetilde{v_{i}}=0
otherwise. Also, let
a=|\{i\,|\,\text{$v_{i}\neq 0$ and $i\not\in\textsf{supp}(M)$}\}|
be the number of nodes in the support of
\mathbf{v}
that are not contained in the hypermatching
M
. Then note that
T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}=T_{\tilde{\mathbf{v}},h-a,M}
. If we show that
(q^{n}/|B|)\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\tilde{\mathbf{v}},h-a,M}}|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})|\leq U_{C,s}(h-a)
then, by the monotonicity of
U_{C,s}(\cdot)
in the interval
[1,s]
(see
6.4
), it follows that
(q^{n}/|B|)\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}}|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})|\leq U_{C,s}(h)
. Thus, from now on, we assume
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{v})\subseteq\textsf{supp}(M)
.

Step 1: A partition of
T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}
.

We now further refine
T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}
, i.e., the set of non-zero Fourier coefficients. For an integer
\ell
, let
T_{\mathbf{v},h,\ell,M}=\{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}\,|\,\#\{i\in[m]\,|\,e_{i}\cap\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\not=\emptyset\}=\ell\}
be the set
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\mathbf{v},h,\ell,M}
’s such that the support of
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
touches exactly
\ell
edges. Since
\mathbf{v},h
and
M
will be fixed in the rest of this proof, we simplify the notation and refer to this set as
T_{\ell}
. Note that
h/k\leq\ell\leq\min\{m,h\}
. Thus, the quantity we are interested in this lemma can be upper bounded as follows.

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in S_{h}}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{\min\{m,h\}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{\min\{m,h\}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sqrt{|T_{\ell}|\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}}

(6.7)

where the second equality is due to
6.1
, the third equality is due to the partition, and the last inequality is by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. (The reason why we partition
T_{\mathbf{v},h,M}
into
T_{\ell}
’s is that the Fourier square-mass within
T_{\ell}
and the cardinality of
T_{\ell}
can be properly upper bounded respectively.)

Step 2: Upper bounding the squared Fourier mass within
T_{\ell}
.

To upper bound the squared Fourier mass within
T_{\ell}
, we utilize the fact that the posterior set
B
is independent to the choice of center
\mathbf{c}
(i.e.,
6.6
) and hypercontractivity (i.e.,
Lemma

2.12
). We stress that in the bound we establish below
it is crucial that the exponent of
b
is
h-\ell
(as opposed to the more trivial
h
, or
h(k-1)/k
). In turn this bound is obtained by using a random center
\mathbf{c}
and this randomization is permitted at the analysis stage by
Lemma

6.6
.

6.8
.

Let
\zeta_{1}=\zeta
be the constant from
Lemma

2.12
. If
|B|\geq q^{n-b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{N}
then for every
1\leq\ell<h\leq b
, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}\leq k^{\ell}\left(\frac{|B|}{q^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}\cdot b}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}\,.

The proof of this claim uses
Lemma

6.1
to relate the Fourier coefficients of the function
\mathbf{1}_{B}
to those of
\mathbf{1}_{B_{r}}
. But note that the “reduced set”
B_{r}
depends on the choice of the center. Furthermore the weight of the Fourier coefficient in the reduced space depends on how the centers overlap with
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})
. Specifically we have that for centers
\mathbf{c}
,
\|\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\|_{0}=\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}-t
, where
t=|\{i\in[m]\,|\,c_{i}\in\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\}|
is the number of centers contained in
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
. Note that
t\leq\ell
since the number centers in
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})
can not exceed the number of edges touching this set. The crux of this proof is that we if choose the centers randomly then there is a positive probability that all centers (of the edges that touch
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})
) are in
\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})
. We argue the formal details below.

Proof of
6.8
.

For a random center
\mathbf{c}
, let
A_{\mathbf{c}}
denote the
\mathbf{c}
-centered folded encoding of
M
, and let
B_{r,\mathbf{c}}=\{A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\,|\,\mathbf{x}\in B\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}
.
For
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}
, let
I_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{c})=1
if
c_{i}\in\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})
for every
i\in[m]
with
e_{i}\cap\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\neq\emptyset
and
0
otherwise. Note that
\Pr_{\mathbf{c}}[I_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{c})=1]\geq k^{-\ell}
. Now consider the following expression:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{c}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\\
\|\mathbf{w}+\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\cdot\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h-\ell\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,\mathbf{c}}}}(\mathbf{w})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{c}}\left[\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}I_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{c})\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle(\because~\autoref{lem:fourier coeff of set})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\left(\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{c}}\left[I_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{c})\right]\right)

\displaystyle(\because B\text{ and }T_{\ell}\text{ are independent to }\mathbf{c})

\displaystyle\geq k^{-\ell}\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}\,.

\displaystyle(\because\Pr_{\mathbf{c}}[I_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{c})=1]\geq k^{-\ell})

Rearranging the above we get

\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}\leq k^{\ell}\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{c}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\\
\|\mathbf{w}+\widetilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\cdot\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h-\ell\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,\mathbf{c}}}}(\mathbf{w})^{2}\right].

(6.9)

On the other hand, since
B
is
(M,\mathbf{c})
-restricted, we have
|B|=|\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\ |\ A_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{x}\in B_{r,\mathbf{c}}\}|=|B_{r,\mathbf{c}}|\cdot q^{n-\textsf{rank}(A_{\mathbf{c}})}
. As
\textsf{rank}(A_{\mathbf{c}})=(k-1)m
and
|B|\geq q^{n-b}
, we have that
|B_{r,\mathbf{c}}|=|B|/q^{n-(k-1)m}\geq q^{(k-1)m-b}
. Hence, by
2.12
(invoked with
n\leftarrow(k-1)m
and
B\leftarrow B_{r,\mathbf{c}}
), for every
\mathbf{c}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\\
\|\mathbf{w}+\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\cdot\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h-\ell\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,\mathbf{c}}}}(\mathbf{w})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{|B_{r,\mathbf{c}}|}{q^{(k-1)m}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}b}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}\,,

where
\zeta_{1}=\zeta
is the constant from
Lemma

2.12
.
Also, by
|B|=|B_{r,\mathbf{c}}|\cdot q^{n-(k-1)m}
, the above inequality becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\\
\|\mathbf{w}+\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\cdot\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h-\ell\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,\mathbf{c}}}}(\mathbf{w})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{|B|}{q^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}\cdot b}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}\,.

Taking expectations over
\mathbf{c}
we thus get:

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{c}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(k-1)m}\\
\|\mathbf{w}+\tilde{A}_{\mathbf{c}}\cdot\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h-\ell\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{r,\mathbf{c}}}}(\mathbf{w})^{2}\right]\leq\left(\frac{|B|}{q^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}\cdot b}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}\,.

(6.10)

Putting the inequalities
Eq.

6.9
and
Eq.

6.10
together we get

\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}\leq k^{\ell}\left(\frac{|B|}{q^{n}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}\cdot b}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell},

thus proving the claim.
∎

Step 3: Upper bounding the cardinality of
T_{\ell}
.

Next, we turn to bounding the size of the set
T_{\ell}
. To do so we explore the structure of the vectors in
T_{\ell}
. We start with some notation. Let

E=\{i\in[m]\,|\,\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\equiv 0\pmod{q}\}

and
O=\{i\in[m]\,\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\pmod{q}\}
. Given a vector
\mathbf{u}
, we define
W_{e}=W_{e}(\mathbf{u})=(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\odot\left(\sum_{i\in E}\mathbf{e}_{i}\right)
and
W_{o}=W_{o}(\mathbf{u})=(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\odot\left(\sum_{i\in O}\mathbf{e}_{i}\right)
, where
\odot
is used to denote the Hadamard product (entrywise product) of two vectors.
Let
\eta
denote the number of edges touched by
W_{e}
and let
o
denote the number of edges touched by
W_{o}
.
Note the following conditions hold when
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}
.

6.11
.

If
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}
, then all the following conditions hold: (1)
|O|\leq h
, (2)
\eta+o=\ell
, and (3)
\eta\leq h/2
.

Proof.

We prove each of the individual claims below:

1.

Note that
\langle\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\pmod{q}
for every
i\in O
, since
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\equiv 0\pmod{q}
and
\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\not\equiv 0\pmod{q}
. Therefore,
|\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\cap e_{i}|\geq 1
for every
i\in O
, implying that
|O|\leq\sum_{i\in O}|\textsf{supp}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})\cap e_{i}|\leq\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h
.

2.

Since
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
touches
\ell
edges,
\eta+o=\ell
.

3.

Note that
\langle\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\equiv 0\pmod{q}
for every
i\in E
, since
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\equiv 0\pmod{q}
and
\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{e}_{i}\rangle\equiv 0\pmod{q}
. Therefore, if
i\in E
is touched by
W_{e}
(i.e.,
W_{e}\odot\mathbf{e}_{i}\neq\mathbf{0}
), then it follows that
W_{e}
touches it in at least two points, i.e.,
|\textsf{supp}(W_{e}\odot\mathbf{e}_{i})|\geq 2
(see
Figure 3
). Combined with the fact that
|\textsf{supp}(W_{e})|\leq\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h
, we obtain
\eta\leq h/2
, as desired.

∎

Figure 3:
Upper bound the cardinality of
T_{\ell}
. In this example,
q=3,k=5,m=5
, and
\mathbf{v}
is specified by integers in
red
. Note that
E=\{2,3,5\}
and
O=\{1,4\}
. Next, we consider
h=8,\ell=4
and a
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}
specified by integers in
blue
. Note that by definition we have
\eta=o=2
. In particular, the tuple
(W_{e}(\mathbf{u}),W_{o}(\mathbf{u}))
is described on the right and
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
is specified by integers in
green
. It is immediate to see that
(W_{e}(\mathbf{u}),W_{o}(\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{v})
uniquely specifies
\mathbf{u}
because one can subtract
W_{e}(\mathbf{u})
and
W_{o}(\mathbf{u})
by
\mathbf{v}
to get the value of
\mathbf{u}
in those coordinates. In the rest of the coordinates,
\mathbf{u}
has the same values as
\mathbf{v}
. Moreover, observe that every hyperedge in
W_{e}(\mathbf{u})
should contain at least
2
non-zero points because both
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
sum up to
0
mod
q
within those hyperedges.

Based on these restrictions on
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}
, we can now get the following bound on
|T_{\ell}|
.

6.12
.

For every
\ell\in\{h/k,\ldots,\min\{h,m\}\}
we have:

|T_{\ell}|\leq(4q^{2k})^{h}(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}},\mbox{~~~~ where }\eta^{*}:=\min\{\ell,h/2\}\,.

Proof.

Recall that each
\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}
is uniquely specified by the pair
(W_{e},W_{o})
(see
Figure 3
) and it therefore suffices to count the number of distinct choices of
(W_{e},W_{o})
. First, we see that the number of possibilities for
W_{o}
is at most
(q^{k})^{|O|}\leq q^{kh}
(since
|O|\leq h
by the first item of
6.11
). Now, having fixed
W_{o}
and
o
, consider the number of possibilities of
W_{e}
. We may choose
W_{e}
by picking a set
F\subseteq E
with
\eta
edges, and then picking
|\textsf{supp}(W_{e})|=h-|\textsf{supp}(W_{o})|
elements from the union of the edges in
F
, each of which is given a value in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus\{0\}
. Note that
F
can be chosen in at most
\binom{|E|}{\eta}\leq\binom{m}{\eta}
ways, after which
W_{e}
can be chosen in
\leq q^{k\eta}
ways. Finally, note that by the second and third items of
6.11
we have
\eta\leq\min\{\ell,h/2\}=:\eta^{*}
. Putting these together we get:

|T_{\ell}|\leq\sum_{\eta=0}^{\eta^{*}}\left\{q^{kh}\binom{m}{\eta}q^{k\eta}\right\}\leq q^{2kh}\sum_{\eta=0}^{\eta^{*}}\binom{m}{\eta},

where the second inequality uses
\eta\leq h
. Now we consider two cases based on whether
\eta^{*}\leq m/3
or not. If
\eta^{*}>m/3
, since
\eta^{*}\leq\ell\leq m
we have

\sum_{\eta=0}^{\eta^{*}}\binom{m}{\eta}\leq\sum_{\eta=0}^{m}\binom{m}{\eta}=2^{m}\leq 2^{2h}\,,

where the final inequality uses
\min\{\ell,h/2\}>m/3
to infer
m<3h/2<2h
.
Thus in this case we get

|T_{\ell}|\leq q^{2kh}4^{h}=(4q^{2k})^{h}\leq(4q^{2k})^{h}(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\,,

where the final inequality above uses
em\geq m\geq\eta^{*}
.
In the case,
\eta^{*}\leq m/3
we note that
\binom{m}{\eta}\geq 2\binom{m}{\eta-1}
for every
\eta\in\{0,\ldots,\eta^{*}\}
and thus
\sum_{\eta=0}^{\eta^{*}}\binom{m}{\eta}
is a telescoping sum bounded by
2\binom{m}{\eta^{*}}
which in turn is bounded by
2(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\leq 2^{2h}(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}
. Again the desired bound on
|T_{\ell}|
follows.
∎

Step 4: Completing the proof of
Lemma

5.17
.

The boundedness of
B
now follows from some straightforward (though tedious) calculations.
Continuing with the RHS of
Eq.

6.7
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{\min\{h,m\}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sqrt{|T_{\ell}|\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T_{\ell}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{\min\{h,m\}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{|B|}{q^{n}}\right)^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

(By
6.8
and
6.12
, and using
b\leq s
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{\min\{h,m\}}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

\displaystyle=S_{1}+S_{2}

where

S_{1}:=\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}},

and

S_{2}:=\sum_{\ell=h/2+1}^{\min\{h,m\}}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}.

Using
\eta^{*}=\ell
for the regime in
S_{1}
and
\eta^{*}=h/2
in the
S_{2}
regime we can simplify the above two sums as follows:

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(em/\ell)^{\ell}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{(4ekq^{2k})^{h}\cdot(m/\ell)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{(4ekq^{2k})^{h}\cdot(m/\ell)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{2\zeta_{1}s}{h}\right)^{h-\ell}}~~~\mbox{(Using $\ell\leq h/2$)}

\displaystyle=(8\zeta_{1}ekq^{2k})^{h/2}\left(\frac{s}{h}\right)^{h/2}\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{\left(\frac{mh}{2\zeta_{1}s\ell}\right)^{\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq(8\zeta_{1}ekq^{2k})^{h/2}\left(\frac{s}{h}\right)^{h/2}\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{\left(\frac{km}{2\zeta_{1}s}\right)^{\ell}}\mbox{~~~(Using $\ell\geq h/k$)}

\displaystyle\leq(8\zeta_{1}ekq^{2k})^{h/2}\left(\frac{s}{h}\right)^{h/2}\sum_{\ell=h/k}^{h/2}\sqrt{\left(\frac{kn}{2\zeta_{1}s}\right)^{\ell}}\mbox{~~~(Using $m\leq n$)}

\displaystyle\leq(8\zeta_{1}ekq^{2k})^{h/2}\left(\frac{s}{h}\right)^{h/2}\cdot 2\cdot\left(\frac{kn}{2\zeta_{1}s}\right)^{h/4}\mbox{~~~(Using $s\leq\varepsilon_{0}n$. See footnote.)}

\displaystyle=2(2^{5/2}\zeta_{1}^{1/2}ek^{3/2}q^{2k})^{h/2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{sn}}{h}\right)^{h/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{h}\left(\frac{\sqrt{sn}}{h}\right)^{h/2}\,,

for
C_{1}\geq\sqrt{2^{7/2}\zeta_{1}^{1/2}ek^{3/2}q^{2k}}
.

We now turn to simplifying
S_{2}
. We have

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=h/2+1}^{\min\{h,m\}}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(em/\eta^{*})^{\eta^{*}}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=h/2+1}^{\min\{h,m\}}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(2em/h)^{h/2}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}\mbox{~~~(Using $\eta^{*}=h/2$ in this regime)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=h/2+1}^{h}\sqrt{(4q^{2k})^{h}\cdot(2em/h)^{h/2}\cdot k^{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq(8ekq^{2k})^{h/2}(m/h)^{h/4}\sum_{\ell=h/2+1}^{h}\sqrt{\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h-\ell}\right)^{h-\ell}}

\displaystyle=(8ekq^{2k})^{h/2}(m/h)^{h/4}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{h/2-1}\sqrt{\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{\ell^{\prime}}\right)^{\ell^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq(8ekq^{2k})^{h/2}(m/h)^{h/4}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{h/2-1}\sqrt{\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h/2}\right)^{\ell^{\prime}}e^{h/2}}\mbox{~~~(Using $(x/y)^{y}\leq(x/z)^{y}\cdot e^{z}$ for every $x>0$ and $y,z\geq 1$)}

\displaystyle\leq(8e^{2}kq^{2k})^{h/2}(m/h)^{h/4}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{h/2-1}\sqrt{\left(\frac{\zeta_{1}s}{h/2}\right)^{\ell^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq(8e^{2}kq^{2k})^{h/2}(n/h)^{h/4}\zeta_{1}^{h/2}4((2s)/h)^{h/4}\mbox{~~~(Using $m\leq n$, $\zeta_{1}\geq 1$ and $h\leq s$)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}^{h}\left(\frac{\sqrt{sn}}{h}\right)^{h/2}\,,

for
C_{2}\geq(2^{8}\zeta_{1}e^{2}kq^{2k})^{1/2}
.
Combining the bounds on
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
we get

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_{0}=h\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|=\frac{q^{n}}{|B|}\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in S_{h}}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B}}(\mathbf{u})\right|\leq S_{1}+S_{2}\leq(C_{1}^{h}+C_{2}^{h})(\sqrt{sn}/h)^{h/2}\leq C^{h}(\sqrt{sn}/h)^{h/2}\,

for
C\geq\max\{2,C_{1}+C_{2}\}
.

Thus, we conclude that
B
is
(M,C,s)
-weakly-reduced for every
s\in[b,\varepsilon_{0}n]
, and so by
Lemma

6.5
,
B
is
(M,C_{0},s)
-strongly-reduced for every
s\in[b,\varepsilon_{0}n]
.
This concludes the proof of
Lemma

5.17
.
∎

6.4
Proof: boundedness implies near uniformity

In this section we prove
Lemma

5.18
which is used to prove
condition (iv) of
Lemma

5.1
.
We restate the lemma below for convenience.

See
5.18

In the following, we denote
m=\alpha n
for simplicity. Let us start with defining a combinatorial quantity
p(h,k,m,n)
and showing an upper bound on it.

6.13
.

Suppose
k,m,n>0
are integers. We define
p(h,k,m,n)
to be the probability that a uniformly random
k
-hypermatching
M
on vertex set
[n]
with
m
hyperedges each of size
k
, the support of
M
contains
[h]
and further satisfies the condition that every hyperedge of
M
contains either
0
or at least two vertices from
[h]
.

6.14
[
L
]
emma 6.8.

CGSV21-conference]
